22 OCTOBER 2026 · COMPLETE SUPPORTING EVIDENCE

Qwen 27B and Flash-Next on Strata

Every scored question, retained final answer, expected result, rubric and grading explanation. Public edition accompanying the article on mayos.dev.

GPU tests · Conclusion · Markdown edition · Evaluation archive · Back to article · All objective inputs · Review sources and rubric

Public supporting edition. All 87 scored prompts, 174 objective answers, five review tasks and all original/retry review answers are preserved, alongside the rubrics and 18 follow-up speed answers. Unrelated application integration records and host process dumps are omitted. Original strict grades remain frozen; the separate content audit credits both workflow sets at 24/24. Paths in method snapshots are generalized; recorded protocol hashes identify original snapshots, while manifest.json hashes the public files. Publication provenance.

Full GPU comparison, original baseline, sample ranges, settings and every follow-up speed answer. The expert cache adapts during these small sequential samples. Production files and portal PID were unchanged.

Methodology and limitations

This report covers the October 22, 2026 comparison on one active workstation: Ryzen 9 9950X, 96 GB installed RAM (owner-reported; about 91 GiB OS-visible), two RTX 5060 Ti 16GB cards (PCIe 5 x8 and PCIe 3 x1). Qwen3.8-27B Q4_K_M ran on llama.cpp b11476; Flash-Next IQ3_S ran on Strata 0.1.47, release v0.2.2. Both used 131072 context tokens, one active request and both GPUs; cache precision differed (f16 versus int8). Strata split 41/7 layers. This compares complete configurations, not an isolated engine effect. The benchmark Strata bundle lacked the optional jsonschema package: the external fixed grader checked the expected schema-case content. Neither configuration of this Strata release provides grammar-constrained decoding.

Both configurations received the same fixed 87 cases: 24 synthetic workflows, 3 retrieval probes and 60 MMLU-Redux 2.0 professional_law questions. The law sample is the first 60 rows annotated ok; flagged rows were excluded before either scored pass. Neither candidate is ground truth. It is a fixed sample, not a representative legal-accuracy estimate; training exposure is possible. All inputs are text and no real firm documents or tools were used.

Context and attachments: the main comparison used no uploaded PDFs, DOCX files, images or OCR. Contracts, invoices, emails and other source records were supplied inline as text in the task prompt. Each case started a fresh request with a short system instruction plus that question and its supplied records; candidates did not receive this chat, previous test answers, firm files, grader keys, reference anchors or review rubrics. The 131072-token window is a capacity limit, not the amount supplied to every question. The only multi-turn exceptions were two tool cases, which appended the candidate's call and a fixed synthetic document/search result. Complete reconstructed API message arrays, tool definitions, schema requirements and continuations are linked per case in requests/. Normal model-specific chat templates also format those messages; fully rendered token streams were not retained.

Internet and research: neither candidate had browser, web-search or internet-retrieval tools in the comparison. The two supplied tool functions read fixed synthetic fixtures; they never searched the internet. Academic law questions used the candidate's learned knowledge and reasoning. Source-grounded tasks used only the records supplied in the prompt. The host's internet connection was used to obtain models, software and the public question dataset; this did not give the inference requests research tools. These results do not measure research-enabled performance or citation verification against external sources.

The later application acceptance is separate. Its Word test did attach a synthetic DOCX to the application. The application extracted paragraph text and editability metadata for the model, under the firm's normal instructions and the Word editing instructions; the model did not read the DOCX binary or rendered page images. The application rendered and checked preservation independently. The synthetic original, reviewed document, extracted paragraphs and generated plans are retained in integration/.

Objective generation used thinking enabled, medium effort, temperature 1, top-p .95, top-k 20, presence penalty 0, seed 42, one pass. Output budgets include reasoning and final answer: 2048 workflow, 3072 law, 1024 retrieval tokens. Failed requests and truncated outputs receive no credit. Plain-text proofreading must match exactly. JSON content must match expected values and types; one surrounding JSON fence can earn content credit but fails bare-JSON compliance. Tool cases require one exact call and arguments, followed by correct use of a fixed synthetic result.

After prose appeared before some law answers, a post-hoc choice audit was uniformly applied to both models: take the parsed answer object, or the final answer-only JSON object at the end of the response. Request failures and truncations cannot earn credit. Original grades remain unchanged. This distinguishes selecting the right letter from following the required output format.

A second post-hoc audit gives workflow content credit after normalizing optional square brackets around a single-letter source label, uniformly for both models. Both score 24/24 for workflow content. Flash’s redline-effect answer correctly gives 45 days and source [B], equivalent to B. Its original exact-field score remains 23/24; all original grades and totals remain frozen. This does not imply exact-string compatibility for software consuming that field. The audit retains every original and normalized value in records/workflow-content-audit.json.

Five novel source-grounded review tasks used reference anchors and a rubric written before generation. The assistant in the chat reviewed randomized A/B final answers, saved all grades, then revealed model labels. No independent API judge or human reviewer was used. Dimensions are grounding 4, completeness 3, instruction following 2, clarity 1. Reference facts are anchors, not mandatory wording. One initial contract answer hit its 3072-token ceiling; before reading any answers, both models were retried on that task at 4096. Its retry pair was scored; the other four pairs retain original answers. Original and retry answers are all included below. Qwen's retry still truncated.

Speed used three repetitions at each of three input lengths, exactly 128 output tokens, thinking disabled, temperature .7, top-p .8, top-k 20, presence penalty 1.5, seeds 700–702. All cached-prompt counts were zero. The game was closed and downloads/preparation finished; other desktop applications and VMs remained. Loading and warmup, application queue, extraction and browser rendering are excluded. Generation improved 3.69–6.08x; short 128-token reply latency improved 1.46–2.38x. Four review tasks both models completed were 6.26–12.70x faster; these descriptive task times also include different reasoning/output lengths. Restarts took about 30–36 seconds and may benefit from OS file cache.

Tokens are small chunks of text. Generation tokens/s measures how fast the model writes after processing the input. Complete reply time includes input processing, generation and small client/server overheads. The 128-token cap fixes answer length, not question length: input sizes were 84, 6586 and 26087 tokens. Faster writing alone need not mean a sooner complete answer.

Separate reasoning-channel text was deliberately discarded. Final answers, including any explanations delivered as final content, are preserved. Original speed-test answer text was deliberately not retained: its timings, token counts and requests are included, but missing text is not reconstructed or presented as an observed answer. Request bodies below are reconstructed from the frozen harness and fixed fixtures, not captured network traffic. Tool continuation bodies use the retained actual call IDs and arguments.

Preliminary probes and the superseded original-MMLU exploration are excluded from headline scores. The original MMLU exploration was stopped after documented answer-key concerns; the audited Redux sample replaced it before both scored passes. Initial Qwen objective timings overlapped downloads and are not controlled speed evidence. Preliminary Strata CPU-memory sampling missed worker-thread children; its CPU memory totals are invalid. Corrected controlled-run memory sampling found about 49 GiB host PSS for Flash versus 4.3 GiB warmed Qwen; much of Flash's footprint is reclaimable mapped pages, and both exclude VRAM. Global swap growth occurred during preparation and both runtimes.

CPU and RAM dependence: the follow-up startup logs show a CPU expert pool of 15 workers on the 16-core Ryzen 9 9950X; other expert work is cached or computed on the GPU. CPU compute, memory access and PCIe transfers all contribute. CPU utilization percentage and isolated CPU/RAM scaling were not measured. Follow-up sampled peak host PSS was 47.13 GiB on one GPU and 53.80 GiB in the fresh two-GPU control, excluding VRAM and including reclaimable mapped weights. Those observed footprints are not minimum-RAM requirements. Strata can discard mapped pages and reread them from storage, with a performance cost. Logs preserve the adaptive cache and CPU/PCIe decisions in gpu-comparison/*.placement-and-cache.txt.

All four original configuration hashes matched when the comparison ended; the temporary servers were stopped and prior service activity restored. Later application deployment is outside this public report. This public edition accompanies the published article. Dataset attribution: Edinburgh Dataset Analytics Working Group, MMLU-Redux 2.0, CC BY 4.0, https://huggingface.co/datasets/edinburgh-dawg/mmlu-redux-2.0.

Review rubric

{
  "grounding": {
    "max": 4,
    "description": "4: no factual errors; 3: minor ambiguity; 2: a material unsupported claim but the main result is correct; 1: a major error affecting the recommended action; 0: multiple major fabrications. Claims must trace to supplied sources; conflicts and missing facts remain explicit."
  },
  "completeness": {
    "max": 3,
    "description": "3: covers core requested issues and material qualifications; 2: one important omission; 1: multiple important omissions; 0: not responsive. Reference facts are anchors, not required wording."
  },
  "instructionFollowing": {
    "max": 2,
    "description": "2: follows scope, format, length and source-trust rules; 1: minor format/length departure; 0: major prohibited change or action."
  },
  "clarity": {
    "max": 1,
    "description": "Clear, concise and useful to the intended reader."
  }
}

Questions, answers and grading

invoice-extract · extraction

Prompt

Record [A]: Invoice INV-4831, issued 2026-04-11 by Cedar Labs to Alder LLC; subtotal $4,800; tax $384; total $5,184; due 2026-05-11. Return JSON with invoice_id, issuer, total_cents, due_date (ISO).

Expected answer

{
  "invoice_id": "INV-4831",
  "issuer": "Cedar Labs",
  "total_cents": 518400,
  "due_date": "2026-05-11"
}

Qwen 27B / llama.cpp

15.18 s · finish: stop · original grade: pass

{"invoice_id":"INV-4831","issuer":"Cedar Labs","total_cents":518400,"due_date":"2026-05-11"}

Grading explanation

The original grade passes. Record A supplies INV-4831, Cedar Labs, $5,184 (518400 cents), due 2026-05-11.

{
  "passed": true,
  "strictJson": true,
  "expected": {
    "invoice_id": "INV-4831",
    "issuer": "Cedar Labs",
    "total_cents": 518400,
    "due_date": "2026-05-11"
  },
  "actual": {
    "invoice_id": "INV-4831",
    "issuer": "Cedar Labs",
    "total_cents": 518400,
    "due_date": "2026-05-11"
  }
}

Flash-Next / Strata

5.04 s · finish: stop · original grade: pass

```json
{
  "invoice_id": "INV-4831",
  "issuer": "Cedar Labs",
  "total_cents": 518400,
  "due_date": "2026-05-11"
}
```

Grading explanation

The original grade passes. Record A supplies INV-4831, Cedar Labs, $5,184 (518400 cents), due 2026-05-11. Bare-JSON formatting fails. The fixed grader accepts a single JSON fence for content, but does not extract objects following prose; the separate law-choice audit does.

{
  "passed": true,
  "strictJson": false,
  "expected": {
    "invoice_id": "INV-4831",
    "issuer": "Cedar Labs",
    "total_cents": 518400,
    "due_date": "2026-05-11"
  },
  "actual": {
    "invoice_id": "INV-4831",
    "issuer": "Cedar Labs",
    "total_cents": 518400,
    "due_date": "2026-05-11"
  }
}

contradiction · grounding

Prompt

Use only these records; neither has priority. [A] Signed statement: delivery occurred May 2, 2026. [B] Signed statement: delivery occurred May 4, 2026. Return JSON with dates (both ISO dates sorted), conflict (boolean), and resolved_date (null if not resolved).

Expected answer

{
  "dates": [
    "2026-05-02",
    "2026-05-04"
  ],
  "conflict": true,
  "resolved_date": null
}

Qwen 27B / llama.cpp

16.82 s · finish: stop · original grade: pass

{"dates":["2026-05-02","2026-05-04"],"conflict":true,"resolved_date":null}

Grading explanation

The original grade passes. The equal-priority statements give May 2 and May 4. Preserve both dates and the conflict; no resolved date is supplied.

{
  "passed": true,
  "strictJson": true,
  "expected": {
    "dates": [
      "2026-05-02",
      "2026-05-04"
    ],
    "conflict": true,
    "resolved_date": null

      "2026-05-04"

redline-effect · document-reasoning

Prompt

[A] Original: Payment is due within 30 days. [B] Accepted edit: delete "30", insert "45". [C] Pending unaccepted edit: delete "45", insert "60". Report the operative period in days as JSON days, operative_source. Unaccepted edits have no effect. Source is the record label.

Expected answer

{
  "days": 45,
  "operative_source": "B"
}

Qwen 27B / llama.cpp

22.55 s · finish: stop · original grade: pass

{"days":45,"operative_source":"B"}

Grading explanation

The original grade passes. Accepted edit B makes 45 days operative; the pending 60-day edit is not accepted. The raw key uses B; [B] identifies the same source and is a formatting-only difference. The separate post-hoc workflow content audit passes: optional square brackets around a source label do not change the identified record. The original exact-field grade is preserved.

{

  "strictJson": true,
  "expected": {
    "days": 45,
    "operative_source": "B"
  },
  "actual": {
    "days": 45,
    "operative_source": "B"
  }
}

The separate post-hoc workflow content audit passes: optional square brackets around a source label do not change the identified record. The original exact-field grade is preserved. B. Yes, the defendant did not receive a fair trial; the testimony of the women only showed his bad character but because they were old incidents and not continuing, they did not allow a permissible inference that these affairs gave him a motive to kill his spouse.

D. No, the testimony was admissible because it tended to show a pattern of "transferred intent" that proved the defendant's great hostility to his wife, and his need to eliminate her from his life so that he could go back to having a wild life.

mmlu-redux-law-19 · public-law

Prompt

Answer this academic professional-law multiple-choice question.
A passenger on a subway car placed her pocketbook on the seat next to her and began to zip up her jacket. Defendant was standing in front of the passenger and holding on to the hand rail. Seeing the pocketbook unattended, he suddenly grabbed it and tried to run away. As he did so, the passenger became very frightened and fell backward. She struck her head against the window and was knocked unconscious. In the commotion, Defendant dropped the pocketbook and hurriedly exited the subway car empty-handed. Defendant should be found guilty of
A. larceny.
B. attempted larceny.
C. assault.
D. robbery.
Return only JSON {"answer":"A"} with the single best choice letter A, B, C or D.

Expected answer

{
  "answer": "A"
}

Qwen 27B / llama.cpp

126.70 s · finish: stop · original grade: fail

Looking at this question, I need to determine which crime best fits the facts.

**Key facts:**
- Pocketbook was in the passenger's immediate presence (on the seat next to her)
- Defendant suddenly grabbed it while she was right there (zipping jacket)
- The sudden action caused her to become "very frightened," fall backward, strike her head, and be knocked unconscious
- He momentarily carried it before dropping it and fleeing

**Analysis:**

- **Larceny (A):** Requires a trespassory taking and asportation with intent to permanently deprive, but without force or intimidation against the person. Here, the taking was from the victim's immediate presence and caused her to be frightened and physically harmed, elevating it beyond simple larceny.

- **Attempted larceny (B):** The asportation (carrying away) was actually completed when he grabbed and began running with the pocketbook. The crime was completed before he dropped it.

- **Assault (C):** While she was frightened, the primary criminal act was the taking of property. The fear was a byproduct of the property crime, not a separate assault.

- **Robbery (D):** Robbery = larceny + force or intimidation, taken from the person or **presence** of the victim. The pocketbook was in her presence. The sudden, forceful snatch caused her to be "very frightened," fall, and be knocked unconscious. The intimidation/force element is satisfied by the suddenness of the grab in her immediate presence causing fear and physical harm. The asportation was completed when he grabbed and began running with it.

The sudden snatch from the victim's presence, causing fear and physical injury, constitutes robbery.

{"answer":"D"}

Grading explanation

The original grade fails. Selected choice: D; human-reviewed dataset key: A. Choice audit: incorrect. This explains answer-key grading; no additional legal rationale is invented. Bare-JSON formatting fails. The fixed grader accepts a single JSON fence for content, but does not extract objects following prose; the separate law-choice audit does. Compare the actual parsed value and expected value below; raw grades were not rewritten.

  "passed": false,
  "strictJson": false,
  "expected": {
    "answer": "A"
  },
  "actual": null

A. $205,000.00
B. $225,000.00

Qwen 27B / llama.cpp

136.76 s · finish: stop · original grade: fail

Looking at this contract damages question, I need to determine which costs are recoverable in a breach of contract action.

**Recoverable costs (mitigation and loss):**
- $200,000 in losses from the breach (despite mitigation efforts) — recoverable as the core damages
- $5,000 broker fee to find substitute logs — recoverable as a reasonable cost of mitigation
- $25,000 transportation costs for substitute logs — recoverable as a reasonable cost of mitigation

**Non-recoverable costs:**
- $2,000 attorney's fee — Under the American Rule, attorney's fees are NOT recoverable in contract cases (absent a specific contractual provision or statutory exception). This fee was for general advice about options, not a necessary cost of mitigation.

**Calculation:**
$200,000 + $5,000 + $25,000 = $230,000

The mill's duty to mitigate was satisfied by its efforts (finding substitute logs, paying for transport), and those reasonable mitigation costs are recoverable in addition to the net loss sustained.

As she did so, the defendant picked up the check and left the bank before she could say anything. The check that the defendant presented at the bank was not recovered by law enforcement authorities and was not offered as evidence. A. inadmissible, because the witness is at fault in allowing the loss of the original by failing to secure the check.
B. inadmissible, because it is not possible for the jury to compare the signature on the check since it was not presented as evidence.
C. admissible, because it is rationally related to the witness's perception.
D. admissible, because the witness was familiar with the alleged victim's handwriting.

  "answer": "D"
}

A. The fisherman, because when he used the channel he believed that it was a public waterway.
B. The fisherman, because he caused no damage to the man's land.
C. The man, but recovery is limited to nominal damages for the fisherman's intentional use of the channel.

Which of the following offers for the sale of widgets is not enforceable if the seller changes his mind prior to acceptance?
A. A merchant tells buyer in writing she will sell the widget for $35,000 and that the offer will be irrevocable for ten days.
B. A merchant writes buyer offering to sell the widget for $35,000.
C. A merchant telegraphs buyer offering to sell the widget for $35,000 and promises to hold the offer open for ten days.
D. A merchant writes buyer offering to sell the widget for $35,000 and stating that the offer will be irrevocable for ten days if buyer will pay $1.00. Buyer pays.
Return only JSON {"answer":"A"} with the single best choice letter A, B, C or D.

  "answer": "B"

Qwen 27B / llama.cpp

197.42 s · finish: stop · original grade: fail

Looking at this question, I need to identify which offer the seller can revoke (i.e., is NOT enforceable as irrevocable) under the UCC and common law.

**Option A:** Merchant's signed writing + assurance of irrevocability for 10 days → Enforceable under UCC § 2-205 (Firm Offer). Cannot be revoked.

**Option B:** Merchant's simple written offer, no assurance it will be held open, no consideration → This is a plain, revocable offer. However, this is basic contract law.

**Option C:** Merchant telegraphs offer + promises to hold open for 10 days. Under UCC § 2-205, a firm offer requires a **"signed writing."** A telegram does NOT constitute a signed writing. Even though the merchant made the promise to hold it open, the form requirement is not met. Without consideration and without a signed writing, this promise to keep the offer open is unenforceable. The seller CAN revoke.

**Option D:** Merchant's written offer + buyer pays $1.00 → This creates an **option contract** supported by consideration. Enforceable. Cannot be revoked.

The key distinction being tested is the "signed writing" requirement of UCC § 2-205. Option C has all the substantive elements (merchant, promise to hold open, time period) but fails the formal requirement because a telegram is not a signed writing.

mmlu-redux-law-64 · public-law

Prompt

Answer this academic professional-law multiple-choice question.
A woman was sailing in a bay when she was struck by a motorboat. The woman, who was seriously injured in the accident, sued the motorboat operator in a personal injury action. During the course of the lawsuit, an important question of consequence was the wind speed on the afternoon the woman was sailing. At trial, the woman calls an orthopedic surgeon to testi1'. The surgeon, who is an amateur boating enthusiast, testified that he was navigating his sailboat into the bay when the motorboat hit the woman. Furthermore, the surgeon testified that in the cockpit of his boat he maintains a sophisticated electronic weathering device that measures wind speed, temperature, and barometric pressure at periodic intervals. The woman then offers into evidence the computer printout from the surgeon's weathering device measuring the wind speed at the time of the accident. Upon objection by the motorboat operator, the printout is
A. admissible as past recollection recorded.
B. admissible as the record of regularly conducted activity.
C. inadmissible as hearsay not within any recognized exception.
D. inadmissible, unless there is foundation testimony as to the accuracy and good working condition of the surgeon's electronic weathering device on the afternoon in question.
Return only JSON {"answer":"A"} with the single best choice letter A, B, C or D.

{
  "answer": "D"
}

Qwen 27B / llama.cpp

95.02 s · finish: stop · original grade: fail

Looking at this evidence question, I need to determine the admissibility of a computer printout from an electronic weather device.

**Key Analysis:**

The printout is being offered for the truth of the matter (the actual wind speed at the time of the accident), making it hearsay.

- **Option A (Past recollection recorded):** Inapplicable. The surgeon is present and testifying. This exception requires the witness to be unable to recall.

- **Option B (Regularly conducted activity):** While the device records at "periodic intervals" (suggesting regularity), the critical issue is that the surgeon's testimony only establishes that he *maintains* the device and that it records periodically. He did NOT testify that the device was operating accurately or in good working condition on the specific afternoon of the accident. For machine-generated records to be admitted, foundation must be laid showing the device was functioning properly.

- **Option C (Inadmissible as hearsay with no exception):** This is too absolute. The printout *could* qualify under a hearsay exception (regularly conducted activity) if proper foundation were established.

- **Option D (Inadmissible unless foundation as to accuracy/good working condition):** This correctly identifies the problem. The surgeon's testimony about maintaining the device and its periodic recording is insufficient. Additional foundation testimony is needed establishing the device was in good working condition and accurate on the specific afternoon in question. Without that foundation, the printout cannot be admitted.

The answer is D.

Actual requests and retained answers
Speed and auxiliary records
18 speed receipts and request links · Retained warmup answers · Excluded original-MMLU exploration
Original speed answer text was not retained. It is not reconstructed; the later GPU follow-up retains its speed answers. Separate private reasoning was discarded; explanations emitted as final content remain in the answers.
Conclusion
On this workstation, one RTX 5060 Ti on the PCIe 5 ×8 slot was sufficient for the tested workload. It retained 84–87% of the fresh two-GPU generation rate and completed the 128-token replies 18–43% sooner. The second card on PCIe 3 ×1 offered a modest generation gain without improving those full-reply times.
This supports using one GPU for these short-reply workloads. We did not isolate the bus’s contribution or test a faster second slot. Longer reasoning-heavy replies and single-GPU accuracy were not retested.
Sources: MMLU-Redux 2.0, Edinburgh Dataset Analytics Working Group, CC BY 4.0. Full model provenance and hashes are in the results archive. See bundle file hashes.
